Meet my friend Suhail: just two years ago, he quit his corporate consulting job to start a business that now generates $1.1 million a year. In this video, we’ll unpack exactly how he pulled it off. If you’ve ever dreamed of quitting your job to chase freedom, travel, and maybe even work from your laptop on a beach somewhere, stick around - Suhail’s 12-step playbook might just be your ticket. Hope you enjoy xx

00:07:44 --> 00:07:48
running, I'll usually have the outline

00:07:46 --> 00:07:50
as bullet points in notion or in Google

00:07:47 --> 00:07:52
Docs anyway. So then all I have to do is

00:07:49 --> 00:07:54
copy and paste the bullet points into

00:07:51 --> 00:07:55
Gamma and it will automatically create a

00:07:53 --> 00:07:57
first draft of the slides for me. I have

00:07:54 --> 00:07:59
to find my own Aliabadal House style in

00:07:56 --> 00:08:00
these slides so it can look and feel the

00:07:58 --> 00:08:01
way I want them to by default. And then

00:07:59 --> 00:08:03
I can go through and make any changes I

00:08:00 --> 00:08:04
want and it just saves hours and hours

00:08:02 --> 00:08:06
compared to trying to do the

00:08:03 --> 00:08:07
same thing in Google Slides or in

00:08:05 --> 00:08:09
PowerPoint or in Keynote. Gamma also

00:08:06 --> 00:08:10
works really nicely for exploring and

00:08:08 --> 00:08:12
visualizing ideas. So, one thing I'll

00:08:09 --> 00:08:13
often do is that I'll go for a walk.

00:08:11 --> 00:08:15
I'll dictate a voice note into Voice

00:08:12 --> 00:08:16
Pal. And then I'll take the transcript

00:08:14 --> 00:08:18
of that voice note and chuck it straight

00:08:15 --> 00:08:19
into Gamma. And this is really helpful

00:08:17 --> 00:08:21
when I'm trying to sort of visually

00:08:18 --> 00:08:22
ideate for a new video that I'm working

00:08:20 --> 00:08:23
on or I've actually been doing this for

00:08:21 --> 00:08:25
the new book that I'm writing. There's

00:08:22 --> 00:08:26
omething about turning the text into a

00:08:24 --> 00:08:28
slideshow and then being able to sort of

00:08:25 --> 00:08:29
just go through the slides where I can

00:08:27 --> 00:08:31
sort of imagine myself talking through

00:08:28 --> 00:08:33
them and explaining the idea that often

00:08:30 --> 00:08:35
gives me way more clarity on how good

00:08:32 --> 00:08:36
the idea is and what bits need work

00:08:34 --> 00:08:37
compared to just reading the thing out

00:08:35 --> 00:08:39
on a Google doc. So, if you need to do

00:08:36 --> 00:08:40
any kind of presentation for your work

00:08:38 --> 00:08:42
or for your business or your side hustle

00:08:39 --> 00:08:43
or even just for fun, you should

00:08:41 --> 00:08:44
efinitely check out Gamma. And if you'd

00:08:42 --> 00:08:46
like to check it out, you can head over

00:08:43 --> 00:08:48
to gamma.app. So, thank you Gamma for

00:08:46 --> 00:08:51
sponsoring the video. And let's get back

00:08:47 --> 00:08:51
to it.

00:08:53 --> 00:08:58
All right. So, over a 5-year period of

00:08:55 --> 00:08:59
working in corporate consulting, Sahale

00:08:57 --> 00:09:01
is saving some percentage of his

00:08:58 --> 00:09:02
paycheck each month, and he's building

00:09:00 --> 00:09:04
up his buffer of savings. And this is a

00:09:02 --> 00:09:06
somewhat this this way looking graph

00:09:03 --> 00:09:07
because when he's in a in his first two

00:09:05 --> 00:09:09
years he's not earning very much but

00:09:06 --> 00:09:10
hen there's a big like salary jump hit

00:09:08 --> 00:09:12
3 four and 5 years and so eventually he

00:09:09 --> 00:09:13
gets his savings to the point where he's

00:09:11 --> 00:09:16
got enough to cover a year worth of

00:09:12 --> 00:09:16
expenses.

00:09:17 --> 00:09:21
At this point 5 years in he quits his

00:09:18 --> 00:09:23
job so he no longer has any income and

00:09:20 --> 00:09:25
now he has to eat into his savings. If

00:09:22 --> 00:09:26
you keep your lifestyle costs low, then

00:09:24 --> 00:09:27
those savings can last you a certain

00:09:25 --> 00:09:29
amount of time. But for some people, the

00:09:26 --> 00:09:30
lifestyle costs are very high and so

00:09:28 --> 00:09:32
that savings don't last very long. And

00:09:29 --> 00:09:33
then everyone has a sort of kind of

00:09:31 --> 00:09:35
threshold where they're like, "Oh [ __ ]

00:09:32 --> 00:09:37
if my savings get below this threshold,

00:09:34 --> 00:09:38
that is too much for me at that point."

00:09:36 --> 00:09:39
And then what I have to do is go back

00:09:37 --> 00:09:41
and find another job, go back to

00:09:38 --> 00:09:42
corporate, like whatever, whatever the

00:09:40 --> 00:09:43
situation might be. Now, there's a

00:09:41 --> 00:09:45
couple of things to flag here, which is

00:09:42 --> 00:09:47
basically how high up this line is and

00:09:44 --> 00:09:48
also the gradient of your burn. These

00:09:46 --> 00:09:50
are the two things that basically

00:09:48 --> 00:09:51
determine your runway, which is

00:09:49 --> 00:09:53
basically how much time can you give

00:09:50 --> 00:09:54
yourself to work on your business to try

00:09:52 --> 00:09:56
and get it off the ground. Before you

00:09:53 --> 00:09:58
either run out of money or before your

00:09:55 --> 00:10:00
savings that you're eating into dip

00:09:57 --> 00:10:02
below your threshold of comfortableness.

00:09:59 --> 00:10:03
In Sahel's case, when he quit his job,

00:10:01 --> 00:10:05
he tried his best to lower his expenses

00:10:02 --> 00:10:07
o he had as much runway as possible.

00:10:04 --> 00:10:09
And he also decided that emotionally

00:10:06 --> 00:10:10
speaking, he was happy to lose 12 months

00:10:08 --> 00:10:13
worth of living expenses from his

00:10:10 --> 00:10:14
avings in order to give himself that

00:10:12 --> 00:10:15
chance to start his business, that thing

00:10:13 --> 00:10:17
that he'd been dreaming of doing for

00:10:14 --> 00:10:18
such a long time. For some people, the

00:10:16 --> 00:10:19
thing is going to be 3 months or 6

00:10:17 --> 00:10:21
months or 12 months or 2 years or 5

00:10:18 --> 00:10:22
years or even 10 years depending on a

00:10:20 --> 00:10:23
how much savings you've got and

00:10:21 --> 00:10:25
epending on how much money you're

00:10:22 --> 00:10:26
comfortable losing and depending on your

00:10:24 --> 00:10:27
kind of lifestyle cost. So obviously if

00:10:25 --> 00:10:28
you have loads of kids and you have an

00:10:26 --> 00:10:30
expensive lifestyle then you're going to

00:10:27 --> 00:10:31
have a lot less runway than someone who

00:10:29 --> 00:10:32
doesn't have any kids for example. So

00:10:30 --> 00:10:34
while you're in this period of runway

00:10:32 --> 00:10:35
while you're dipping into your savings

00:10:33 --> 00:10:37
your goal is to try and start a business

00:10:34 --> 00:10:38
uch that you actually you know it

00:10:36 --> 00:10:39
starts to make money and then things

00:10:37 --> 00:10:41
tarts to take off or at least starts to

00:10:38 --> 00:10:43
experience enough momentum that you

00:10:40 --> 00:10:44
realize okay I'm going to go all in on

00:10:42 --> 00:10:45
this thing. Now, the thing to mention

00:10:43 --> 00:10:47
here is that if you're going to follow

00:10:44 --> 00:10:48
this particular method, if you are going

00:10:46 --> 00:10:50
to take the plunge method, as I call it,

00:10:48 --> 00:10:52
of just diving into the deep end and

00:10:49 --> 00:10:53
just straight up quitting your job

00:10:51 --> 00:10:55
without a plan in order to try and get a

00:10:52 --> 00:10:57
business off the ground, you have to be

00:10:54 --> 00:10:59
comfortable dipping into your savings.

00:10:56 --> 00:11:00
This is a thing that holds so many

00:10:58 --> 00:11:04
people back. I have friends and team

00:10:59 --> 00:11:05
embers who have like 50k to 100k USD

00:11:03 --> 00:11:07
worth of savings, and that could be like

00:11:04 --> 00:11:08
3 to 5 years worth of runaway based on

00:11:06 --> 00:11:09
their living cost because they don't

00:11:07 --> 00:11:11
have kids and things. But there is this

00:11:08 --> 00:11:12
psychological hurdle as soon as they

00:11:10 --> 00:11:14
start dipping into their savings and

00:11:11 --> 00:11:16
they're like, "Oh my god, dipping into

00:11:13 --> 00:11:18
my savings is a terrible fate. I

00:11:15 --> 00:11:21
couldn't possibly. What if I need those

00:11:17 --> 00:11:23
savings when I'm 95 years old to try and

00:11:20 --> 00:11:25
like buy me an extra 2 days of life or

00:11:22 --> 00:11:27
like that?" There'll be some kind of

00:11:24 --> 00:11:30
thought process where it's like there's

00:11:26 --> 00:11:31
this emotional overwhelm and fear around

00:11:29 --> 00:11:32
ipping into the savings. Some of that

00:11:30 --> 00:11:34
fear is a practical concern. You know,

00:11:31 --> 00:11:35
it's useful to have money in the bank in

00:11:33 --> 00:11:37
cases like life emergencies and stuff,

00:11:34 --> 00:11:39
but quite a lot of it in my experience

00:11:36 --> 00:11:41
is the emotional fear factor around

00:11:38 --> 00:11:42
ipping into the savings. Now, if Sahel

00:11:40 --> 00:11:44
had had an emotional fear factor around

00:11:41 --> 00:11:45
ipping into his savings, none of this

00:11:43 --> 00:11:47
would have ever happened. He would still

00:11:44 --> 00:11:48
be working his job that he hated in

00:11:46 --> 00:11:50
corporate consulting. But because he

00:11:47 --> 00:11:51
actively decided to invest in himself

00:11:49 --> 00:11:52
and sort of treated himself as his own

00:11:50 --> 00:11:54
startup where he's like, "All right,

00:11:52 --> 00:11:56
cool. I'm going to give myself 12

00:11:53 --> 00:11:57
months. I've got 12 months worth of

00:11:55 --> 00:11:59
runway where I don't need to worry about

00:11:56 --> 00:12:01
making money." And that is the time, the

00:11:58 --> 00:12:02
timebound amount of time I'm going to

00:12:00 --> 00:12:04
give myself to get this business off the

00:12:01 --> 00:12:05
ground. If it doesn't work, I'm just,

00:12:03 --> 00:12:07
well, at least I'll have given it a go.

00:12:04 --> 00:12:08
I'm just going to go back to consulting,

00:12:06 --> 00:12:10
you know, with my tail between my legs.

00:12:07 --> 00:12:11
And that's fine because at that point,

00:12:09 --> 00:12:13
I'm no longer going to have the what if.

00:12:10 --> 00:12:15
What if I'd taken that plunge? What if

00:12:12 --> 00:12:16
I'd invested in myself? What if I'd

00:12:14 --> 00:12:18
taken a bet, taken that risk to start

00:12:15 --> 00:12:21
hat business? Why did you decide to

00:12:17 --> 00:12:23
straight up quit the job uh rather than

00:12:20 --> 00:12:25
uh side hustle on the evenings and the

00:12:22 --> 00:12:26
weekends alongside the day job to sort

00:12:24 --> 00:12:29
of try and get your thing off the

00:12:25 --> 00:12:32
ground? So, I did try that. Um, I did

00:12:28 --> 00:12:34
this very unconventional thing of doing

00:12:31 --> 00:12:36
part-time at a consulting company, which

00:12:33 --> 00:12:39
is quite rare because it's quite quite a

00:12:35 --> 00:12:41
full-on job. I was doing 4 days a week

00:12:38 --> 00:12:43
instead of five for a few months, and

00:12:40 --> 00:12:45
that came with a pay cut. So, it was a

00:12:42 --> 00:12:47
bit of a small risk. I tried that and it

00:12:44 --> 00:12:50
did not work because I think a full-time

00:12:46 --> 00:12:52
job just takes over your headsp space

00:12:49 --> 00:12:55
and you don't really have enough time to

00:12:52 --> 00:12:56
think of your own business. At least I

00:12:54 --> 00:12:58
didn't. I'm sure other people, you know,

00:12:56 --> 00:13:01
there will be people who can, but for

00:12:57 --> 00:13:04
me, it just did not work. And it was the

00:13:00 --> 00:13:06
moment where I quit the job to actually

00:13:03 --> 00:13:09
go all in on something that I felt like

00:13:05 --> 00:13:11
I had the space to experiment with a few

00:13:08 --> 00:13:13
things. And to be honest, I had a bit of

00:13:10 --> 00:13:15
a pressure to make make something out of

00:13:12 --> 00:13:17
it, you know? And without that, without

00:13:14 --> 00:13:19
having that kind of uh pressure and

00:13:16 --> 00:13:20
space to do it, I don't think I would

00:13:18 --> 00:13:23
have gotten this far. I probably would

00:13:19 --> 00:13:25
have had a smaller side hustle that like

00:13:22 --> 00:13:27
you can't half your way into that kind

00:13:24 --> 00:13:28
of thing, right?

00:13:26 --> 00:13:30
Yeah. There's a lot of people that sort

00:13:27 --> 00:13:33
of sell the dream around like, oh, you

00:13:29 --> 00:13:34
could get to 10K a month by just sort of

00:13:32 --> 00:13:36
working in the working in the weekends,

00:13:33 --> 00:13:38
working in the evenings. Um, but it

00:13:35 --> 00:13:39
sounds like that that didn't really work

00:13:37 --> 00:13:40
for you. And don't get me wrong, there

00:13:38 --> 00:13:42
are people who do that. Like there are

00:13:39 --> 00:13:44
people who will on the weekends really,

00:13:41 --> 00:13:46
you know, be in full hustle mode.

00:13:43 --> 00:13:48
They'll be selling courses or doing

00:13:45 --> 00:13:50
coaching or that kind of thing. But I

00:13:47 --> 00:13:52
just found it didn't work for me. Yeah,

00:13:49 --> 00:13:54
that's fair enough. So, Hel tried the

00:13:51 --> 00:13:55
side hustling idea working in management

00:13:53 --> 00:13:57
consulting. There was a load of work.

00:13:54 --> 00:13:58
Um, he told me afterwards that even

00:13:56 --> 00:14:00
though he was technically working 4 days

00:13:57 --> 00:14:01
a week, he still had like 6 days a week

00:13:59 --> 00:14:02
worth of work. And so, the part-time

00:14:00 --> 00:14:04
xperiment didn't really work. But, you

00:14:01 --> 00:14:05
know, kudos to him for trying. Um, and

00:14:03 --> 00:14:07
again, I know a bunch of people who are

00:14:04 --> 00:14:08
in a similar situation as Sahel where

00:14:06 --> 00:14:09
they've got a job that they don't

00:14:07 --> 00:14:11
particularly like. They could go

00:14:08 --> 00:14:13
part-time, but they don't even try.

00:14:10 --> 00:14:14
Like, they don't think it's possible to

00:14:12 --> 00:14:16
go part-time. And it's like, you know,

00:14:13 --> 00:14:17
technically it wasn't really possible in

00:14:15 --> 00:14:18
Sahil's job, but he found a way to

00:14:16 --> 00:14:20
hustle for it, to negotiate for it. And

00:14:17 --> 00:14:21
because he was good at his job, they

00:14:19 --> 00:14:22
didn't want to lose him. And so they

00:14:20 --> 00:14:24
were quite happy to allow him to work

00:14:21 --> 00:14:26
technically 3 4 days a week. Anyway, he

00:14:23 --> 00:14:28
tried the experiment of shallow dipping

00:14:25 --> 00:14:29
toes, high side hustling. That

00:14:27 --> 00:14:31
experiment did not work. So he was like,

00:14:28 --> 00:14:32
"All right, cool. I'm going to plunge in

00:14:30 --> 00:14:35
on the deep end. I'm going to quit the

00:14:31 --> 00:14:36
job." So you stick it out at consulting

00:14:34 --> 00:14:37
for 5 years so that you get your

00:14:35 --> 00:14:39
permanent residence in the UK. And it

00:14:36 --> 00:14:42
just really opens up the world for you.

00:14:38 --> 00:14:43
Um and so my understanding is that on

00:14:41 --> 00:14:45
the day that you got the ability to live

00:14:42 --> 00:14:48
in the UK permanently, you decided to

00:14:44 --> 00:14:50
quit the job. Yeah. Um for me that was

00:14:47 --> 00:14:53
my moment of freedom. So actually I was

00:14:49 --> 00:14:55
quite riskaverse and I didn't quite quit

00:14:52 --> 00:14:58
my job immediately. I negotiated a

00:14:54 --> 00:15:00
one-year um sabbatical kind of leave of

00:14:57 --> 00:15:02
absence with my employer. It's it's

00:14:59 --> 00:15:04
basically like unpaid. So it's just an

00:15:01 --> 00:15:06
offer to come back in a year's time,

00:15:03 --> 00:15:08
right? So it's not like they're paying

00:15:05 --> 00:15:11
you to take a year out. So yeah, that

00:15:07 --> 00:15:13
was my risk averse person's way of doing

00:15:10 --> 00:15:15
the quit your job and you know go off on

00:15:12 --> 00:15:18
your own thing. How did you negotiate a

00:15:14 --> 00:15:21
year-long sobatical? So the company I

00:15:17 --> 00:15:23
worked at do do like 3 month breaks.

00:15:20 --> 00:15:25
That's quite a common thing because it's

00:15:22 --> 00:15:27
quite a full-on job and people will

00:15:24 --> 00:15:29
after a couple years or so take a few

00:15:26 --> 00:15:32
months out to do that kind of thing. I

00:15:28 --> 00:15:34
hadn't done that for the 5 years. So uh

00:15:31 --> 00:15:37
I kind of had some brownie points in my

00:15:33 --> 00:15:39
favor. It was also kind of a recession

00:15:36 --> 00:15:41
year, so I think my employer was glad

00:15:38 --> 00:15:44
that I was off the payroll for a year

00:15:40 --> 00:15:45
anyway. So, it kind of worked out. Um,

00:15:43 --> 00:15:47
yeah, that was basically it. So, even

00:15:44 --> 00:15:48
though he quit his job, he was actually

00:15:46 --> 00:15:50
de-risking the whole thing by again

00:15:48 --> 00:15:51
doing a good job of negotiating a

00:15:49 --> 00:15:52
year-long sobatical where they were

00:15:50 --> 00:15:54
like, you know what, a year from now,

00:15:51 --> 00:15:55
you can just come back at the same

00:15:53 --> 00:15:58
salary and at the same position. Nice.

00:15:54 --> 00:16:00
Okay. So, 5 years in, you get the

00:15:57 --> 00:16:02
passport, you quit the job, and you're

00:16:00 --> 00:16:03
like, all right, I've got this year.

I've got a year of runway. I'm going to

00:16:02 --> 00:16:08
invest in myself. I'm going to do the

00:16:03 --> 00:16:10
thing. Great. What happens next? Like,

00:16:07 --> 00:16:14
yeah. So, I immediately went on holiday

for uh so for a month, I was just like,

I'm kind of burnt out. I just want some

00:16:15 --> 00:16:24
time to relax and get my head clear from

00:16:19 --> 00:16:27
this past consulting life and uh you

00:16:23 --> 00:16:30
know, just kind of turn over a new page.

00:16:26 --> 00:16:33
And yeah, so I did three four weeks of

that. uh went to like a beach town for a

week or so, came back and was just

00:16:34 --> 00:16:39
chilling for a while. And I think it was

like three or four weeks into that that

00:16:38 --> 00:16:44
I began to get that itch of I'm not

00:16:41 --> 00:16:46
doing anything with my life. I need to

00:16:43 --> 00:16:48
make something. I need to be productive.

00:16:45 --> 00:16:50
And yeah, I think that's around the time

00:16:47 --> 00:16:52
when you and I chatted about, you know,

00:16:49 --> 00:16:54
what kind of business could uh could I

00:16:51 --> 00:16:56
work on and, you know, what should I do

with my newfound freedom? And it was at

00:16:55 --> 00:16:59
his point that me and Sahel and two

00:16:56 --> 00:17:01
ther friends went out for dinner and we

00:16:58 --> 00:17:03
re talking about like okay what kind

00:17:00 --> 00:17:06
of business can he start. So now we come

to step two and do the reading. So I

asked Sahel what he remembered from that

dinner that we had 2 years ago. Yeah. So

I remember that quite clearly. Uh we

00:17:10 --> 00:17:16
re at dinner with our friends Nate and

Sam and at the time I was like mate I

just quit my job. I'm going to go all in

on all the hobbies I've been doing in

this last 5 years that I haven't had the

time to properly do. and uh you were

00:17:26 --> 00:17:32
like, "Okay, so what are you gonna work

on?" And I think at the time I was like,

you know, music's always been a kind of

passion of mine and I've never really

path of entrepreneurship and actually

you and kind of being a bit more

00:30:10 --> 00:30:17

00:30:12 --> 00:30:20

omething that people would actually pay

00:30:22 --> 00:30:27
for. That was my kind of mentality on

00:30:25 --> 00:30:29
focusing on problems rather than

00:30:26 --> 00:30:31

commonest mistakes I see beginners who

00:30:30 --> 00:30:33

00:30:36 --> 00:30:40

arrived on the entrepreneurship scene,

00:41:41 --> 00:41:47
uh, The Sweaty Startup by Nick Huber.

00:41:43 --> 00:41:48
How to get rich doing boring things. Um,

00:41:46 --> 00:41:49
it's relatively new. It's very good.

00:41:47 --> 00:41:51
I've been recommending this to a lot of

00:41:48 --> 00:41:53
people. His whole shtick, which also

00:41:50 --> 00:41:55
vibes with what Professor Scott Galloway

00:41:52 --> 00:41:57
says around how to make money, is

00:41:54 --> 00:41:59
basically the more boring the idea, the

00:41:56 --> 00:42:00
more likely it is going to work as a

00:41:58 --> 00:42:02
business. Actually, the thing that

00:41:59 --> 00:42:03
here's a there's a fun little line from

00:42:01 --> 00:42:05
here. You want a business with low

00:42:02 --> 00:42:07
status. You want a business that isn't

00:42:04 --> 00:42:08
sexy, exciting, or even a little bit

00:42:06 --> 00:42:10
interesting. You don't care what people

00:42:07 --> 00:42:11
think about you, and you don't care what

00:42:09 --> 00:42:13
people think about your business idea.

00:42:10 --> 00:42:14
I'll give you a few scenarios. Scenario

00:42:12 --> 00:42:17
ne, Nick runs an artificial

00:42:13 --> 00:42:20
intelligence business. Wow. Boo. I.e.,

00:42:16 --> 00:42:21
that sounds exciting and sexy. So, you

00:42:19 --> 00:42:23
don't want to start that kind of

00:42:20 --> 00:42:25
business. Scenario two, Nick invented a

00:42:22 --> 00:42:27
product he is manufacturing.

00:42:24 --> 00:42:29
High-profile, terrible. Scenario three,

00:42:26 --> 00:42:31
Nick cuts grass. Boring. I like it.

00:42:28 --> 00:42:33
Scenario four, Nick stores people's

00:42:30 --> 00:42:35
junk. Perfect. Basically, the sexier and

00:42:32 --> 00:42:36
more interesting sounding the business

00:42:34 --> 00:42:39
idea sounds when you tell normal people

00:42:35 --> 00:42:41
about it, the harder it is generally,

00:42:38 --> 00:42:43
broadly, to make that kind of business

00:42:40 --> 00:42:44
work. It's also true of like jobs.

00:42:42 --> 00:42:46
Something Scott Galloway says is that

00:42:43 --> 00:42:48
he sexier sounding a job sounds, the

00:42:45 --> 00:42:50
harder it is to make money doing it. Uh,

00:42:47 --> 00:42:51
I'm going to be an actor. I'm going to

00:42:49 --> 00:42:53
be a professional musician. I'm going to

00:42:50 --> 00:42:55
be an influencer. These are all like

00:42:52 --> 00:42:57
high status, sexy sounding jobs. Really

00:42:54 --> 00:42:58
hard to make money doing those. I'm

00:42:56 --> 00:42:59
going to be an accountant. I'm going to

00:42:57 --> 00:43:00
be a plumber. I'm going to be a

00:42:58 --> 00:43:02
whatever. It's like, if the job sounds

00:42:59 --> 00:43:04
boring, there's generally less luck

00:43:01 --> 00:43:05
involved in getting rich through the

00:43:03 --> 00:43:07
job. Here we go. If you need to status

00:43:04 --> 00:43:08
test your business, try this. Go to your

00:43:06 --> 00:43:10
grandma or any other older adult and

tell them what you have in mind. If they

say, "Wow, that is such a good idea." It

00:43:11 --> 00:43:15
means it is actually a terrible idea and

00:43:12 --> 00:43:17
highly competitive and nobody has ever

won that game before. If they say, "Oh,

00:43:16 --> 00:43:19
good for you." That means your business

00:43:17 --> 00:43:21
is boring and has been done before and

00:43:18 --> 00:43:22
your odds of success are much higher.

00:43:20 --> 00:43:24
Don't forget you want a business with

00:43:21 --> 00:43:25
low status because this will attract

00:43:23 --> 00:43:27
less competition. The dreamers will

00:43:24 --> 00:43:29
steer clear. People watch too much Shark

ne has succeeded before or if your

o be doing. And if nobody has won, why

there's probably a bit more of the

you know, find what they're complaining

yeah, just just get that first critical

could help inform your your thing. And

with with that consulting idea, if I had

01:27:08 --> 01:27:13
There's this whole thing about when you

01:27:09 --> 01:27:16
have a new product, there are like the

01:27:12 --> 01:27:19
three L's. There's there's lettuce,

01:27:15 --> 01:27:21
logo, learning. Lettuce is money, right?

01:27:18 --> 01:27:23
So, if you have a customer, you have to

01:27:20 --> 01:27:26
get either money from them like you're

01:27:22 --> 01:27:28
charging them. You have to get some

01:27:25 --> 01:27:29

01:27:27 --> 01:27:30
you're like very early and you're

getting their feedback and you give it

01:27:29 --> 01:27:34
o them for free in exchange for you

01:27:31 --> 01:27:36
developing the product and learning or

01:27:33 --> 01:27:38
you get the logo which is like their

01:27:35 --> 01:27:42
testimonial on your homepage. Yeah. So,

01:27:37 --> 01:27:44
I like when you're starting out, um you

01:27:41 --> 01:27:46
Yeah. get even if you're not able to

01:27:43 --> 01:27:47
charge, you you get one of the other two

01:27:45 --> 01:27:50
things and kind of develop your product

01:27:46 --> 01:27:51
over time and get to a point where you

can charge for it. I think these three

01:27:50 --> 01:27:54
L's are pretty cool. I hadn't heard this

01:27:51 --> 01:27:56
kind of framework before, but lettuce,

01:27:53 --> 01:27:57
learning, and logo. A mistake I see with

a lot of people trying to start

businesses is that they try and go

straight for the money straight away.

Whereas actually in the early days,

especially if you don't have

01:28:10 --> 01:28:15

that, after these seven steps, we

ife and work life thing is is a

thing we the culture there is like you

your personal life. like uh for me it's

important to let's say go to the gym in

of thing or if someone has kids um they

back now, I think that's that's fine. I

don't regret making that decision. Like

01:40:22 --> 01:40:28
at this at this point in life, I'm I'm

01:40:24 --> 01:40:29
happy that I, you know, locked in rather

01:40:27 --> 01:40:31
than okay, yes, I would have had a nice

01:40:28 --> 01:40:33
summer with the family, but I can always

01:40:30 --> 01:40:36
have the summer next year so it can

01:40:32 --> 01:40:39
happen again, right? Okay. A lot of

01:40:35 --> 01:40:41
people quit the corporate job because

they want to work less. They have this

01:40:40 --> 01:40:46
dream of like fun, freedom, flexibility,

fulfillment, like being able to go to

01:40:46 --> 01:40:50

an alarm. Oh, I'll be I'll be, you know,

01:40:49 --> 01:40:54
done by work. I, you know, maybe I could

work three days a week so I can like do

music on the side, etc., etc. Um, what's

been your experience of trading a

01:40:59 --> 01:41:04
demanding corporate job for building a

01:41:01 --> 01:41:08
million dollar plus business? I think

01:41:03 --> 01:41:11
this idea of freedom gets misconstrued

01:41:07 --> 01:41:13
quite a lot like financial freedom okay

01:41:10 --> 01:41:16
people want financial freedom they want

01:41:12 --> 01:41:18
passive income but

01:41:15 --> 01:41:21
I think most people think about it as

freedom from there's the idea of freedom

01:41:20 --> 01:41:26
from something like from the job the

01:41:22 --> 01:41:29
handcuffs from the corporate life

01:41:25 --> 01:41:32
whereas um I think it's healthier to

think about it as freedom to freedom to

or freedom to go and travel the world or

explore. And the the from mindset, I

I'm trying to run away from something

and I'm just kind of always trying to

that are coming after me. Whereas I

01:41:50 --> 01:41:55
think um yeah, a better way is to think

about it as okay, freedom to do

something I'm interested in that will

help me feel like I'm doing something

01:41:58 --> 01:42:04
interesting with my life or will have a

01:42:01 --> 01:42:06
happier day-to-day. Um yeah, I don't

know where I read about this freedom

01:42:05 --> 01:42:09
from and to concept. It's definitely not

been kind of defining for me that

if you're looking for freedom, what are

What's your answer to that question?
